The proof ends: "Hence, we get deg C_i|F ≤ deg Δ_{D_i}|F < 2."`; it should be `C_i|F is either empty or a single point of degree one over L = H⁰(F, O_F), in particular geometrically a single point. The application, that the relevant fibre meets the gluing locus in at most one point, needs only this.`. The degree bound deg C_i|F < 2 gives at most one point of degree one, but not that the scheme is nonempty. When D_i is normal the conductor is empty, and the empty scheme is not connected. The following argument, applying Proposition 5.3, uses only that the generic fibre meets the gluing locus in at most one point. Noted by the extraction (diagnostic 4). - **Gap** at Theorem 1.2, p.3, and the last assertion of Theorem 4.8, p.23, with its proof, pp.24–25, in arXiv 1702.04644v2 (13 July 2018, the latest version; its source file is Duke_Final_Revision.tex; PDF SHA-256 2eacf07f…e1bd); the published text, Duke Math. J. 168 (2019), 377–427, was not collated (it affects a stated result). The source says `lim_{q→∞} Ẽ^±/|H₂(G′,c)[q−1]|`; it should be `For G′ not good, replace lim by liminf: liminf_{q→∞, (q,|G′|)=1} Ẽ^±_{F_q(t),q^{2n}}(G,G′)/|H_2(G′,c)[q−1]| ≥ w_G n^{N_{G′}−1}, and likewise with E^± and a smaller constant in Theorem 1.2.`. The proof bounds the ratio from below for each large q. It keeps only the multidegrees n in a set 𝒞 (all n_i large, with prescribed parities) on which the number of Frobenius-fixed components is |H_2(G′,c)[q−1]|, and discards the other components as nonnegative. That gives a lower bound for the liminf. It does not show the limit exists: the discarded components with small n_i and their Frobenius-fixed counts may depend on q, and nothing in the argument controls them. The good cases, where the limits are computed exactly, are unaffected. In Theorem 1.2 the passage from Ẽ to E divides by the positive constants of §2.1, |Aut_{G′}(G)| and A(G,G′), so the lower bound transfers with a smaller constant. The proof's remark that "the second statements of both theorems are the same" should be read that way. Noted by the extraction (items 97, 141 and 143). The continuation makes the remaining obligation precise: by /307–/309 the ordinary limit exists iff the total normalized fixed-component profile is eventually constant. Stable parity fibers themselves require the obstruction test /300–/304; do not assume all stable fibers are independent of q without that test. No admissible-type counterexample is asserted. - **Error** at Definition 3.12 (from [EVW12, §8.4]), p.13; used again in the proof of Theorem 3.13, p.14, in arXiv 1702.04644v2 (13 July 2018, the latest version; its source file is Duke_Final_Revision.tex; PDF SHA-256 2eacf07f…e1bd); the published text, Duke Math. J. 168 (2019), 377–427, was not collated. The source says `tame quotient`; it should be `Let Γ be the maximal prime-to-p quotient of that tame quotient. This Γ is free pro-prime-to-p on δ_1, …, δ_k with δ_1⋯δ_kδ_∞ = 1. Since |F| is prime to p, φ factors through it.`. In characteristic p the tame fundamental group of P¹ minus a finite set S is not pro-prime-to-p. Grothendieck's comparison (SGA 1, XIII, 2.12, which the paper cites) describes only its maximal prime-to-p quotient as the prime-to-p completion of the topological fundamental group. For example, for p ≥ 5 the tame fundamental group of P¹ ∖ {0, 1728, ∞} has as quotients the groups PSL₂(F_ℓ), ℓ ≠ p, of modular covers X(ℓ) → X(1), ramified with indices 2, 3, ℓ, and these orders are divisible by p for suitable ℓ. The definition and the proof of Theorem 3.13 only use homomorphisms to F and its lift F̃_c, whose orders are prime to p. These factor through the prime-to-p quotient, so nothing changes. Noted by the extraction (item 56). J. 168 (2019), 377–427, was not collated. The source says `"Let Q be a global field with a choice of place ∞. Let G be a finite group and G′ a subgroup of G ≀ S_2. Let c be the order 2 elements of G′ with non-trivial image in S_2. Let φ: Gal(Q̄_∞/Q_∞) → G′ be a ramified homomorphism with image ⟨(1,σ)⟩. There is a bijection between {(K,ρ) | … ρ ∈ Sur(G_K^{un,∞}, G) | ρ is type G′} and {isomorphism classes of marked (G′,c) extensions (L,π,m) of Q with infinity type φ}." The proof builds ρ from a marked extension "by projection onto the first factor" and says "It is straightforward to check that these constructions are inverses to each other".`; it should be `Assume that G′ is admissible, or at least that ker(G′ → S_2) projects onto the first factor G. Only then does the projection give a surjection ρ, and both bijections hold. The paper applies the proposition only to admissible G′ (Theorem 4.8 and §7).`. Without this hypothesis the first bijection fails. Take G = C_3, G′ = ⟨(1,σ)⟩ ≅ C_2 and c = {(1,σ)}, and let Q = ℚ with ∞ the real place. A marked (G′,c)-extension with the ramified infinity type φ is an imaginary quadratic field such as ℚ(√−3) with its marking, so the right side is nonempty. The left side is empty: type G′ requires Gal(L̃/ℚ) ≅ G′, of order 2, while L/K is a C_3-extension. The projection onto the first factor of G′ ∩ (G × G) = 1 is not onto G. Noted by the extraction (items 73 and 142). - **Error** at Conjecture 5.1, p.26, in arXiv 1702.04644v2 (13 July 2018, the latest version; its source file is Duke_Final_Revision.tex; PDF SHA-256 2eacf07f…e1bd); the published text, Duke Math. J. 168 (2019), 377–427, was not collated (it affects a stated result). The source says `u∈μ_{Q(μ_{4|G̃′_c|})}`; it should be `Let u be a generator of μ_{Q(μ_{4|G̃′_c|})} (and h ∈ H_2(G′,c)[|μ_Q|]). Equivalently, stratify by the Hom-valued invariant u ↦ I(ρ,u). This matches Theorem 4.8, which takes u_q ∈ Ẑ(1)^×.`. By Lemma 3.11, u ↦ I_{F,π,K}(φ,u) is a homomorphism from μ_L to H_2(F,c)[|μ_K|], so I(ρ,1) = 1 for every ρ. With u = 1 and h ≠ 1 the count in the numerator is zero for every X, so the limit is 0, not 1, whenever H_2(G′,c)[|μ_Q|] ≠ 1. The paper has such cases. For Q = ℚ, where |μ_Q| = 2, its table in §8.2 lists the good pair G = A_4, G′ = ((C_2⁴) ⋊ C_3) ⋊ C_2 [227] with H_2(G′,c) = C_2, and G = A_5, G′ = A_5 ≀ C_2 with H_2(G′,c) = C_2. More generally, for u of order d the invariant only sees h in the image of μ_d. For a generator u, evaluation at u identifies the homomorphisms μ_L → H_2(G′,c)[|μ_Q|] with H_2(G′,c)[|μ_Q|], since |μ_Q| divides |μ_L|, which is the intended stratification. The braces in "H_2(G′,c){|μ_Q|}" are a misprint for the torsion subgroup H_2(G′,c)[|μ_Q|]. Noted by the extraction (items 99 and 144). No claim that every published version was read..

### Xinyi Yuan, "Arithmetic bigness and a uniform Bogomolov-type result", Annals of Mathematics 203 (2026), no. 1 (`PAPER-YUAN-26`)

- **Error** at Theorem 4.19, function-field case, p.101 (with the definition of maximal variation, §1.6, p.16); the same statement is Theorem 4.19 in arXiv v4, p.100, in the author manuscript dated 21 August 2024 (126 pp., the journal's revision date; SHA-256 b36f4860…813e) (it affects a stated result). The source says `"Theorem 4.19. Let K be either a number field or a function field of one variable. Let S be a quasi-projective variety over K. Let π : X → S be a smooth relative curve over S of genus g > 1 with maximal variation. Let α be a line bundle on X of positive degree on fibers of π. Then the following morphisms satisfy the relative Bogomolov conjecture: (1) the S-morphism i_α^m : X^m_{/S} → J^m_{/S} for any m ≥ dim S + 1 …", where (§1.6) maximal variation means that the moduli morphism S → M_{g,R} is generically finite.`; it should be `For a function field K of a curve over k, the hypothesis must be maximal variation of a model over k (equivalently, as the proof says, bigness of the relevant adelic line bundles over k rather than over K), which excludes isotrivial families. With that hypothesis, or for K a number field, the statement stands.`. Counterexample to the function-field case: k algebraically closed of characteristic 0, K = k(t), S = Spec K, X = C₀ ×_k K for a smooth projective curve C₀/k of genus > 1, and α of degree 1. The moduli morphism Spec K → M_{g,K} is a point, hence generically finite, so X → S has maximal variation in the sense of §1.6. But every point of C₀(k) has canonical height 0 in the constant Jacobian, and these points are Zariski dense in X, so part (1) fails for m = 1 = dim S + 1; constant products give the same failure for the other parts. The proof itself notes that "we need the bigness of the adelic line bundles over k instead of over K", which a constant family does not have. The main uniform Bogomolov theorem excludes isotrivial families and is unaffected. Found by the extraction (items 185–189, 262–266). Recorded as `PAPER-YUAN-26/E1`; looked for an existing correction in: Annals of Mathematics article page https://annals.math.princeton.edu/2026/203-1/p02, checked 22 September 2026: no erratum is linked; arXiv 2108.05625: v1 (2021) to v4 (30 April 2024), all earlier than the manuscript read; no later version; X. Yuan's homepage http://faculty.bicmr.pku.edu.cn/~yxy/ (which links the manuscript): no correction listed; web search for an erratum or corrigendum, 22 September 2026: none found; the final typeset Annals text could not be accessed and was not compared.
- **Error** at §4.6.3, statement of the relative Bogomolov conjecture, p.100, in the author manuscript dated 21 August 2024 (126 pp., the journal's revision date; SHA-256 b36f4860…813e). The source says `"Let k be either Z or a field. Let K be a global field over k. Namely, K is a number field if k = Z; K is a function field of one variable over k if k is a field. … Recall that the relative Bogomolov conjecture of [DGH2, Conj. 1.2] asserts that, if dim Y < g, then there is a constant ε > 0 such that Y(L, ε) = {y ∈ Y(K̄) : ĥ_L(ι(y)) ≤ ε} is not Zariski dense in Y."`; it should be `Over function fields the statement needs the usual exclusion of the constant part (for instance, that A has trivial trace or that Y is not constant); [DGH2] formulates the conjecture over number fields.`. For a constant abelian scheme A = A₀ ×_k S and a constant subvariety Y generating it with dim Y < g (for example a constant curve of genus > 1 in its Jacobian), the constant points have canonical height 0 and are Zariski dense, so the conjecture as stated fails for function fields. No result depends on the conjecture itself; its function-field instances in Theorem 4.19 are E1. Found by the extraction (item 182). Recorded as `PAPER-YUAN-26/E2`; looked for an existing correction in: Annals of Mathematics article page https://annals.math.princeton.edu/2026/203-1/p02, checked 22 September 2026: no erratum is linked; arXiv 2108.05625: v1 (2021) to v4 (30 April 2024), all earlier than the manuscript read; no later version; X. Yuan's homepage http://faculty.bicmr.pku.edu.cn/~yxy/ (which links the manuscript): no correction listed; web search for an erratum or corrigendum, 22 September 2026: none found; the final typeset Annals text could not be accessed and was not compared.
- **Misprint** at §A.1, definition of admissible Green functions, p.102, in the author manuscript dated 21 August 2024 (126 pp., the journal's revision date; SHA-256 b36f4860…813e). The source says `"The smooth Green's function g_D is called admissible if the hermitian metric on O(D) defined by ‖1‖ = exp(−g_D) is admissible. The condition is equivalent to (i/π) ∂∂̄ g_D = dµ_Ar − δ_D as currents on C."`; it should be `(i/π) ∂∂̄ g_D = deg(D) dµ_Ar − δ_D.`. Admissibility of a metric on O(D) is defined just above as c₁(O(D), ‖·‖) = deg(D) dµ_Ar, and c₁ of ‖1‖ = exp(−g_D) is (i/π)∂∂̄ g_D + δ_D. The printed equation is the case deg D = 1, for instance D a point. Found by the extraction (item 192). Recorded as `PAPER-YUAN-26/E3`; looked for an existing correction in: Annals of Mathematics article page https://annals.math.princeton.edu/2026/203-1/p02, checked 22 September 2026: no erratum is linked; arXiv 2108.05625: v1 (2021) to v4 (30 April 2024), all earlier than the manuscript read; no later version; X. Yuan's homepage http://faculty.bicmr.pku.edu.cn/~yxy/ (which links the manuscript): no correction listed; web search for an erratum or corrigendum, 22 September 2026: none found; the final typeset Annals text could not be accessed and was not compared.
- **Error** at §1.6, definition of stable curves, p.16, in the author manuscript dated 21 August 2024 (126 pp., the journal's revision date; SHA-256 b36f4860…813e). The source says `"The curve is called stable if it is semistable with arithmetic genus g > 1, and any rational irreducible component of C_k̄ intersects other irreducible components at three or more points."`; it should be `Count special points on the normalization: every rational component must have at least three points of its normalization lying over nodes, counting both branches of a self-node (the Deligne–Mumford condition). Equivalently, a smooth rational component meets the other components in at least three points, and a rational component with a self-node meets them in at least one.`. The printed condition ignores self-nodes. For example, a rational curve with one node attached at one point to a smooth genus-one curve is a stable curve of genus 2 in the usual sense, but its nodal rational component meets the other component in only one point. The paper uses stable reduction and the moduli of stable curves from the literature, so the intended (standard) notion is clear. Found by the extraction (item 1 and report §8). Recorded as `PAPER-YUAN-26/E4`; looked for an existing correction in: Annals of Mathematics article page https://annals.math.princeton.edu/2026/203-1/p02, checked 22 September 2026: no erratum is linked; arXiv 2108.05625: v1 (2021) to v4 (30 April 2024), all earlier than the manuscript read; no later version; X. Yuan's homepage http://faculty.bicmr.pku.edu.cn/~yxy/ (which links the manuscript): no correction listed; web search for an erratum or corrigendum, 22 September 2026: none found; the final typeset Annals text could not be accessed and was not compared.
